Output:  Okay, here's the step-by-step solution:

We need to find the *derivative* of the function `f(x) = x**4 - 2*x**2 + 1`.

The derivative operation finds the instantaneous rate of change. It applies rules term by term.

For the first term, `x**4`: The power rule says that if you have something like `x^n`, its derivative is `(n)*(x**(n-1))`. So, for `x**4`, n=4: Derivative = 4 * x ** (4-1) = 4*x**3.

For the second term, `-2*x**2`: This has a constant multiplier (-2). The power rule applies to `x**2` (n=2): Its derivative is `(2)*(x**(2-1)) = 2*x`. Then multiply by the constant: -2 * (2*x) = -4*x.

For the third term, `+1`: The derivative of a constant value is always zero. So this term disappears when differentiated.

Putting it all together: The derivative function is composed of these results: f'(x) = 4*x**3 + (-4*x) + 0.

The final answer is:

`f'(x) = 4*x**3 - 4*x`

Output:  Okay, let's differentiate \( f(x) = x^{4} - 2 \cdot x^{2} + 1 \).

We need to find the derivative of this function with respect to \(x\), which tells us its instantaneous rate of change.

Here is our step-by-step working:

1.  The derivative exists because \(f(x)\) is a polynomial (sum/difference of terms involving powers of \(x\)).
2.  We can differentiate each term separately and then combine them.
3.  Differentiating the first term (\(x^{4}\)): Using the power rule, \(\frac{d}{dx}(x^n) = n x^{n-1}\), we get \(4x^{3}\).

5.  Differentiating the third term (\(+1\), a constant): The derivative of any constant with respect to \(x\) is zero.
6.  Combining these results: The total derivative is the sum of the individual derivatives, which is \(4x^{3} + (-4x) + 0 = 4x^{3} - 4x\).

The final answer for the derivative is \( 4*x^3 - 4*x \).

Output:  Okay, let's simplify this expression.

The problem asks us to simplify: (x² - 1) / (x - 1)

To start simplifying, we look at the numerator and denominator separately. The numerator is x² - 1. This fits a special pattern called difference of squares. Difference of squares tells us that any expression like A² - B² can be factored as (A + B)(A - B). Here, A is x and B is 1, so we factor it as:
x² - 1 = (x + 1)(x - 1)

Now our expression becomes: ((x + 1)(x - 1)) / (x - 1)
The denominator is exactly one of the factors from the numerator. We can cancel out these common (x - 1) terms, but we have to remember that dividing by zero isn't allowed. The term x - 1 equals zero when x = 1. Since cancelling involves division, our simplified expression cannot be defined at x = 1.

After cancelling, we are left with the single factor from the numerator: (x + 1)

So, the simplified form is:
(x² - 1) / (x - 1) simplifies to (x + 1), for all x except x = 1.

Here's my step-by-step working:

Step 1: Expand the expression using direct multiplication.
\((x + 1) * (x + 1) = x*x \text{ plus } x*1 \text{ plus } 1*x \text{ plus } 1*1\)

Step 2: Calculate each term.
\(x*x = x^2, \quad x*1 = x, \quad 1*x = x, \quad 1*1 = 1\)

Step 3: Combine the like terms (the two 'x' terms).
\(x^2 + x + x + 1 = x^2 + 2x + 1\)

So, the expanded form is \(x^2 + 2x + 1\).

Now, for factoring this expression:

Step 4: Look for common factors. Notice that all terms (\(x^2\), \(2x\), and \(1\)) are divisible by... actually, it's simpler than finding a greatest common factor.

Step 5: Recognize that the expanded form is just the square of \((x + 1)\).
\(x^2 + 2x + 1 = (x)^2 + 2*(x)*1 + (1)^2\)

Therefore, factoring confirms it's equal to its original squared binomial.

The final answer for both steps is \(x^2 + 2x + 1\)

Output:  Okay, little buddy! Let's work on this together.

**Step 1:** We are asked to expand (x+1)**2 using the binomial theorem. That means multiplying (x + 1) by itself once more.

(x + 1)**(2)
= x*x + x*1 + 1*x + 1*1
= x² + x + x + 1

**Step 2:** Now combine like terms from the expansion result.

x² + x + x + 1 = x² + 2x + 1

The expanded form is: **x² + 2x + 1**

Now, we need to factor this expression (x² + 2x + 1).

**Step 3:** Looking at the terms inside the parentheses of the original expansion, we recognize that x² and 1 are both squares. The middle term's coefficient is twice what you'd get if it were just the product of these two base numbers.

So, (x+1)**(2) = **x² + 2x + 1**

Output:  Okay, here's a straightforward breakdown:

1.  We need to first expand `(x + 1)**2` and then factor the result we get.
2.  Expanding `(x + 1)**2` means finding out what it equals when you multiply (x+1) by itself.
3.  So, we compute: `(x + 1)*(x + 1)`.
4.  Using the distributive property (FOIL method), this gives us `x*x = x**2`, `x*1 = x`, `1*x = x`, and `1*1 = 1`.
5.  Adding these terms together results in `x**2 + x + x + 1`.
6.  Combining the like terms (the two 'x' terms) gives us the expanded form: `x**2 + 2*x + 1`.
7.  Factoring this expression means finding a common factor or breaking it down into simpler pieces.
8.  We recognize that `x**2 + 2*x + 1` is exactly what you get when squaring a binomial `(a+b)**2 = a**2 + 2*a*b + b**2`.
9.  Applying this pattern, with `a=x` and `b=1`, gives us the factored form: `(x + 1)**2`.

The final answer is:

`(x + 1)**2`

The problem asks you to solve an equation involving this specific matrix: [[1, 2], [3, 4]]. We need to find the characteristic polynomial and then solve for λ where it equals zero.

To do this, we'll compute the determinant of (A - λI), set it equal to zero, and simplify. Here's how:

Start by forming A - λI: [[1-λ, 2], [3, 4-λ]].
Compute its determinant: det([[1-λ, 2], [3, 4-λ]]) = (1-λ)(4-λ) - (2)(3).
Expand this expression inside the parentheses and subtract: (4 - λ - 4λ + λ²) - 6 → λ² - 5λ + 4 - 6.
Simplify further to get the characteristic polynomial equal to zero: λ² - 5λ - 2 = 0.

Now solve for λ using the quadratic formula. For an equation of the form ax² + bx + c = 0, x = [-b ± sqrt(b² - 4ac)] / (2a). Here a=1, b=-5, c=-2.
Calculate discriminant first: (-5)² - 4(1)(-2) → 25 + 8.
Then take the square root of the discriminant.
Finally compute λ₁ and λ₂.

The eigenvalues are (5 ± sqrt(29)) / 2.

Output:  To find the eigenvalues of a 2x2 matrix [1, 2; 3, 4], solve for λ where det(A - λI) = 0.  
A - λI becomes [[1-λ, 2], [3, 4-λ]].  
The determinant is (1-λ)(4-λ) - (2*3) = λ² -5λ -2.  
Set the equation to zero: λ² -5λ -2=0.  
Solve using quadratic formula: λ=[5±√(25+8)]/2=[5±√33]/2.  
Thus, eigenvalues are (5 + √33)/2 and (5 - √33)/2.

**Final Answer**:  
The eigenvalues of the matrix \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 3 & 4 \end{bmatrix} are λ = \frac{5 + \sqrt{33}}{2} and λ = \frac{5 - \sqrt{33}}{2}.

Output:  Okay, let's find the Laplace transform of \( e^{-2t} \).

The Laplace Transform asks: What integral gives us a function \( F(s) \) such that when we take the inverse Laplace Transform, we get back the original exponential signal \( e^{-2t} \)?

We use the definition. The Laplace Transform is given by:

\[ F(s) = \mathscr{L}\{f(t)\} = \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-st} f(t)  dt \]

Here, our function \( f(t) = e^{-2t} \).

Plugging this into the definition gives us:

\[ F(s) = \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-st} \cdot e^{-2t}  dt \]

Combine the exponential terms in the integral. Since both exponentials are multiplied, we add their exponents:

\[ F(s) = \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-(s + 2)t}  dt \]

This is an integral of \( e^{at} \), but note that here 'a' is actually negative: let's say \( a = -(s + 2) \).

The antiderivative of \( e^{at} \) (where 'a' is constant, and typically for Laplace we want it to be improper integral from 0 to infinity converging requires Re(s)>max(Re(a_i)) or something) but in this case a=-s-2.

We need the antiderivative. The general rule says \( \int e^{at}  dt = \frac{1}{a} e^{at} + C \), provided 'a' is not zero and Re(s)>0 or whatever for convergence, but let's compute formally first.

So, looking at our integral:

\[ F(s) = \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-(s + 2)t}  dt \]

The antiderivative of \( e^{-kt} \), where k=s+2 is a constant for the integration with respect to t.

Using the rule, we get:

Antiderivative: \( -\frac{1}{k} e^{-kt} \)

More precisely, derivative of \( e^{at} \) is 'a' times itself. So integral should be similar but negative because exponent has a minus sign for convergence usually when Re(s)>|Re(a)| or something.

Standard antiderivative: Let u = at, then du=adt, so dt=du/a

Integral becomes ∫ e^u / (something), no wait:

\[ \int e^{-(s+2)t}  dt = -\frac{1}{(s + 2)} e^{-(s + 2)t} + C \]

Yes.

So evaluating the definite integral from t=0 to t=infinity:

Set k = s + 2

Antiderivative is \( -\frac{1}{k} e^{-kt} \) evaluated between 0 and ∞.

At upper limit: lim_{t→∞} \( -\frac{e^{-kt}}{k} \)

Lower limit at t=0: \( -\frac{e^{0}}{k} = -\frac{1}{k} \)

Now, for the integral to converge (which it needs for a Laplace Transform), Re(s) must be greater than 2 so that k is not just negative but has positive real part?
